Manual destinations and orders remain authoritative; mounted movement curves physically and still obeys terrain collision. No speed, stamina or damage bonus. Tests: cavalryride, cavalryplay and mobile freeforallplay.

Cavalry under pursuit and on hard ground (2026-10-27): CavalryRide (commander.js) now gallops clear of a chasing foe it outruns (up to three 150 px legs away from its chasers, chosen to keep off woods, marsh, the field's edge and the battle circle), recovers only with a real gap, and charges again only from a run-up of 60 px or more. A foe as fast as the riders is met rather than stood for; a target that comes at them is charged within 170 px; a moving target's flank route moves with it. Walls (spears of any kind, levies too, and any men in a shield wall or braced) are ridden at from a flank: one leg to a point 115 px beyond the line's last man on its nearer safe flank, then one straight run-in; a wall still facing them there, fighting no one else, makes the riders stand off 6 s rather than charge its points. Anything else is charged straight in, no way round. Legs are ridden at a gallop through their waypoints (no checking between them), and a foe still near as a loop ends is charged at speed rather than after a stop. Charges forced by a corner or an equal pursuer are marked (ridePlan.forced). Chased riders gallop while stamina is 15 or more (otherwise 40). At rest they shadow their mark: they walk to a spot 140 px off his flank on their side (pushed out to 280 px till no foe is near it or their walk there), stand there to get their breath back, and move only when he moves 40 px (CavalryRide.shadow); with no such spot they rest where the ride ended. A foe nearer than a run-up is ridden away from or met (forced), never charged as if from one (CavalryRide.close). Tests: cavalrychase (infantry and cavalry pursuers: run-ups, gaps, gallop share, no brawls), cavalryhard (a moving column, a crowded field, a pike line, woods, a river, hills, the world battle's edge).

Cavalry that fights (2026-10-29, the owner: "cavalry seems to always run away and not attacking"): a charge's riders used to leave after 0.7 s in contact, and at once whenever most of them were near a foe (that is, every time they struck), then rest till 80 stamina for up to 40 s. Now they stay in the melee while it pays (`CAVALRY_RIDE.impact` 4 s) and leave at once only when a spear wall faces them, the foes within `round` (60) px outnumber them `crowd` (1.5) to one, `loss` (1) of them has fallen since they struck, or they are among trees; they rest till 60 for up to 20 s. Measured (38 v 38 in the lab, AI on both sides, 6 seeds): riders in contact 13% → 17% of their time, their share of all damage 17% → 21% (they are 16% of the men). `cavalryride`, `cavalryhard`, `cavalrychase` hold.

Riders that keep moving (2026-11-01, the owner): an AI group's stand that ends its charge is now always a move, its men breaking off to go (battle/battlecontrol.js `standAt`; before, a first stand, or one within `CAPT.move` px of the last, left them in the fight where they were, and riders galloping clear out of woods froze there till their plan ran out). With no foe in sight the captain ends its riders' rides (they finish the leg they are on, and plan anew on sight). `cavalryhard` now takes the runner's `-Seed` for its ground and dice, and allows a forced charge from a full run-up (the world battle's edge counts as a corner); forced and short, one at most. Seeds 1-8: 3 of 8 passed before, 7 of 8 after (seed 2: a pike line given up, as before, and on hills riders cornered in a melee re-charged from close again and again).

Withdrawal controls (2026-10-27): pointing at Withdraw (or a first tap on it on a touch screen, which closes the orders drawer) previews each selected group's way off the field without ordering it (ui/orderpaths.js WithdrawView: the exit morale.js exitChoose or the world battle's circle gives, routed round the ground, "Would leave here" at its end). On a phone the strip under the balance of power offers Retreat now and Cancel; a second tap on Retreat also withdraws; W withdraws at once. While any of yours withdraw, their paths stay on show selected or not, and its strip (WithdrawView.hud) lists them (each a button to select it) with how many are off the field, that the battle ends for you once all are off, and, in a battle of several armies, that the rest fight on without you (also told by the order's toast). Fixed: in a world battle withdrawing men stopped at the circle's edge and never left (the field's ways end there; morale.js withdrawStep now follows the world battle's escape way, as routers do). Tests: withdrawview (desktop and, at a phone's size, the drawer).

Army results (2026-10-27): the battle report opens with every army that fought, yours first (battle/resolution.js ArmyReport: fought, fallen, withdrew, fled, still standing, kills; a world battle's armies as its sides know them, a classic battle's your band, allies and the enemy), what your army earned (the gold, renown, experience and prisoners the settlement actually gave or took), and who was still on the field when your army left (ui/loot.js battleArmiesHtml). Auto-resolve keeps its aggregate summary. Tests: armyresults.

Crowded battles (2026-10-27): a step of a four-army world battle of about 140 men took 47 ms in a headless browser, now about 14–28 ms: the spatial grid empties only its used buckets and searches man by man on a sparse field (identical picks), and a morale group's members are found by group before steadiness. In a press, banners whose badges overlap keep their first UICON.crowd (2) badges, one column (ui/moraleicons.js iconLayout). Tests: battlecrowd (grid exactness on a world field and a crowded lab field, the press, a step's time as INFO).

Unit portrait art (2026-10-29): art/portrait contains one 1254 × 1254 PNG per current ST unit ID: troops, named companions, all four races’ workers and creatures (101 total). hum_sword.png is the unchanged approved style reference. The remaining portraits interpret each unit’s current sprite, equipment and palette in the same detailed pixel-art style. art/portrait/index.html is a searchable, filterable review gallery; portraits.json records names, IDs, tags, filenames, dimensions and checksums. The original unit art remains source material; named-character integration is described below. Verified full roster coverage, unique PNGs, square dimensions, image loading and mobile gallery filters.

Creature portrait poses (2026-10-29): wolf, bear, boar, tiger, griffin and dragon portraits now show natural grounded quadruped bodies and legs, preserving the approved pixel-art palette and lighting. Humanoid and already species-appropriate creature compositions remain. The portrait manifest checksums and gallery image versions are updated.

Named in-game portraits (2026-10-30): ui/characterportraits.js resolves four companions and the twenty founding lords to individual detailed pixel-art faces. Companions use them throughout the Army, hiring and dialogue; lords use their own face in dialogue, the known-lords journal and prison cards. A lord keeps the same face after changing allegiance or entering captivity. Extra generated lords have deterministic varied sprite fallbacks; the player and ordinary troops retain generated portraits. art/portrait/game contains 256 × 256 WebP copies loaded only when displayed; scripts/build-web.py includes those via portraits.json without deploying full PNGs. Tests: portraits, conversation, prisons, structure; deployed image loading and mobile views.

Generated faces (2026-11-07; the owner: "another developer developed a portrait generator: utilize that and include the creation into our game; the lords and named heroes, companions can also use generated portraits; make hair style match with portraits"; `ui/faces.js`, `Faces`, `FACES`). The game loads the portrait engine (`engine/face arts/grayscale/portrait-engine.js`, `portrait-generation.js`: `PortraitEngine`, `PortraitCharacters`) and keeps a roster of faces by stable id in the save (`faces`): yours (the roster's player), a lord's (`lord:` its name; its realm's race, a man or a woman as `pronouns.js` says, its hat or crown, drawn in its realm's colour) and a companion's (`comp:` its id), each made the first time it is shown and never rerolled, unlike yours and every other. The New game page has a face maker under the names (`Faces.maker`; New game is two pages, the owner: "the character creation may split between portrait and background": your hero, his race, names and face, Next; then your past, Back to your hero), with the portrait workshop's controls (the owner: "just make the features from tools like color picking and other features as well"): your portrait; a list for each part of your race's (`FACE_PARTS`: face, ears, hair, eyes, brows, nose, mouth, facial hair, age, marks, clothing, headwear); a colour picker for each tint (`FACE_TINTS`: skin, hair, eyes, lips, cloth, metal, headwear); a slider for each of the engine's eight proportions; a preset of your race; Random face. A list or a preset makes the page again, a picker or a slider only the portrait; changing your race or your hero's gender starts it again from your race's and profile's look. Left alone, one is drawn for you as the game starts. `portrait()` (your hero, a companion) and `lordPic()` show the faces, `FACES.px` a side pixel for pixel, where the paintings were. Your figure and a companion's match their faces (`Faces.look`): the hair's length (`FACE_HAIR` by the engine's `hairGroups`: short a crop, medium to the jaw, long past the shoulders, a bun or topknot its own, none bald) and colour, a beard if the face has one, and no eye drawn, as no soldier has one (the owner: "I don't want eyes") (`engine/doll.js` `cropMid`, `cropLong`, `cropBun`, `beardF`). A companion's face is drawn bareheaded, so the hair shows as on the figure; yours is as you made it (bareheaded unless you choose headwear). An old save without faces gets them drawn as it loads. `FACES.on` false (or the engine missing): the paintings and sprites as before. Tests: `faces`; `structure` (the save's `faces` key).

The dice (2026-11-09; the owner: "Can we add a cool dice animations when rolling something?", then "Use d20 rules for the dice"; `ui/dice.js` `Dice`, `D20`, `DICE`; style.css "the dice"; tests: dice): a check you make is shown as it falls, over the screen, by the d20's rules: a twenty-sided die rolls in, your bonus is added (what your skills give the chance, a point each 5%: `Dice.check(p, label, draw, bonus)`, `Dice.show({p, r, part, bonus})`), and you win on its DC or over ("Rolled 14 + 3 = 17 · DC 12 (60%)"); a natural 20 always wins and a natural 1 always fails, named. The bar under it is the die's twenty faces: green from the face you needed up, amber just under it where a lord's ways win in part, an iron nail where the roll fell; then what came of it (Success, In part, Failed). It waits for you (the owner: "Please wait the player to close the panel, dont auto close"): Continue, a click anywhere, Enter, Space or Esc closes it, and while it is up no other key reaches the game (`Dice.key`, input.js); every suite but `dice` has it off (harness.js `DICE.on=false`). Only the showing: the chance and the one draw are the check's own (the road's and the gates' own streams, Math.random for the rest), so no stream and no fingerprint moves; the die reads them in twentieths (`Dice.face`: the face needed is 21 less your chance's faces, kept to 2..20, the face shown kept on the side the draw fell). The bonuses: a prison's or captor's bribe Persuasion and Trade (`bribe().sk`), breaking out Athletics, slipping away in the rout Riding or Athletics, persuading captors Persuasion, a gate's ways `Gates.mine`, road and story choices their stats (`statPart`), a lord's checks your Persuasion, renown and silver tongue (`LordSpeaker#mine`), the roll to live Medicine. Shown for: every road and story choice with odds (`Choice#roll`), a lord's persuasion, your captivity's persuading, break-out and bribe and slipping away in the rout, a prison's bribe, break-out and asking more for a ransom, a gate's bribe and night's slip, and the roll to live when beasts beat you. `DICE.on` switches it off; the system's reduced motion shows the result still. The die (the owner: "Can we have a dice like this?", a photo of light wooden polyhedrals): `D20`, the solid (twenty flat faces, opposites adding to 21; `D20.rest(d)` lays it with d to you), drawn on a canvas in 3D by `Dice.roll`/`paint`: light haldu wood with its grain (`Dice.wood`, from a hash) turning with each face, lit faces, the numbers burnt in (6 and 9 underlined), thrown in from the left, hopping lower each time; its look and throw in `DICE.die`. The board under it stays the old cracked oak.

Dwarves' faces (2026-11-09; the owner: "Can you make the face a bit more like dwarf?"; `FACE_KIN`, `Faces.kin`): the engine draws a dwarf from faces and noses a human has too, so every dwarf the world draws (lords, companions, kin, a face drawn at random) is drawn by the marks of their people: a dwarven face, the bulb nose, rounded or broad ears, deep-set or heavy eyes, bold brows; a man's thin beard a full or braided one; hair chosen from the shared catalog with racial preferences; the face, jaw and nose broadened, the eyes smaller (each from the face's own seed: the same face every time). Only the drawing: the saved faces are as they were, and a face you made yourself is as you made it.

Faces dressed as they are (2026-11-09; the owner: "Ensure that the characters wearing the correct armor or equipment based on their current armor and make sure lords are having a good armor"; `ui/faces.js` `FACE_GEAR`, `Faces.dress`, `Faces.yours`, `Faces.armoured`): your hero's face (the HUD, the party and character pages, the Family tree, kept as it was at death) and a companion's are drawn in what they wear. Body armour by its tier: wood a padded jack, bronze scale, iron mail, silver brigandine, gold a coat of plates, mithril a steel cuirass, adamant plate; a people's own make where it has one (dwarves' iron guard and rune mail, elves' leaf mail, orcs' lamellar; elves and orcs in steel at the top). A helm likewise (arming cap, coif, nasal, kettle, sallet, bascinet, ridge helm; the dwarves', elves' and orcs' own). The metal is its material's, mixed with steel (`steel`, `tint`). The beasts' pieces: a bear coat fur, a silk vest a jerkin, scorpion plate lamellar, troll hide an orc's hide harness (else fur). A cloak and no armour: a cloak. Nothing worn: never armour or a helm you do not wear (your past's plain clothes, `plain`: a soldier's gambeson, a mage's robe…). The saved face is not changed: only how it is drawn. A lord, who has no gear, is armoured by his standing: silver from level 1, a tier better every 3 levels (`lord: {min, lv}`), his people's make, his realm's colour and his crown kept; his house's kin and its children are not.

Troops' faces (2026-11-09; the owner: "Make sure to change old unit portrait (unitcard) to generated sprite and please use most relevant armor for each unit"; `FACE_UNIT`, `Faces.unit`, `Faces.dressUnit`, `Faces.vOf`): every troop kind's portrait (`portrait(type)`, battlehud.js: its unit card, the barracks, prisoners, guards) is a generated face of its people, dressed as its battle figure is (`lookOf`): its armour as its clothing (plate a knight's plate, mail a squire's mail, leather a levy's gambeson; each people's own make where they have one: a dwarf's iron guard or rune mail, an orc's lamellar or hide harness, an elf's leaf mail or mantle), a caster's robe, a worker's plain clothes; its helm as headwear (a cap an arming cap, a kettle hat, a great helm its people's war helm, a hood, a straw hat); the figure's colours (its coat, its metal, its skin and hair); where the rules miss, the kind's own pair (`FACE_UNIT.own`). A share of each people's troops are women; a hood comes down on an elf or a goblin, so their ears show. The same face in every world (from the kind, never a draw), kept; its card's sprite the same figure (`Faces.vOf`). Beasts keep their paintings; faces off, every kind its painting (the outlaws the old bandits', the alpha a wolf's, `PORTRAIT_LIKE`). Tests: portraits.

Every people as they are (2026-11-09; the owner: "make sure all race or factions use the correct appearance and features on their portrait. Dont forget to have bald"; `FACE_KIN.goblin`, `FACE_RACE`, `FACE_LOOK`, `Faces.tone`): the engine draws four peoples and gave every face of one the same skin and hair; now each face the world draws (lords, their kin, companions, troops) takes one of its people's tones by its seed (`FACE_LOOK.skin`, `.hair`), unless its colours were chosen. A goblin, which the engine lacks, is drawn on an orc's face made small and sharp: a narrow jaw, big pointed ears and eyes, a thin nose, little tusks or a grin, green-yellow (before, a goblin came out human). Bald heads among each people's men (`FACE_LOOK.bald`: humans 14%, dwarves 20%, orcs 30%, goblins 85% and half their women, elves 2%). Your own face and your companions' are as the engine and their people make them (no world tone or bald head: yours you choose in the face maker; theirs is worn by their figures, whose looks the battle's records hold), so the save's, the map's and the battles' fingerprints hold. Tests: portraits.

Animal portraits in game (2026-10-30): bear, wolf, boar, tiger, griffin and dragon now resolve to the corrected natural quadruped artwork through the shared portrait path, including unit cards and battle cards. Their source art was corrected earlier but was not connected to the runtime. Six compact, versioned WebP files are included by the existing deployment manifest. Tests: portraits, deployed asset loading and responsive unit cards.

## Tiger artwork

The approved striped tiger is in `engine/art/tiger/` (master sheet and timing metadata). `tiger-data.js` and `tiger.js`, loaded after the beast engine, supply synchronous pixel frames for the game and wiki. Combat uses the new movement, attack, pounce, hurt and side-fall poses. The falling body is animated from its own frames, without scaling or spinning the standing sprite.

## Wolf artwork

The approved wolf masters and timing data are in `engine/art/wolf/`. `wolf-data.js` and `wolf.js`, loaded after the tiger renderer, replace wild/combat wolf sprites and provide the dedicated eight-frame map walk. Movement, corrected jaw animations and three authored death sequences use the new art. The folded-leg death rests sideways, matching the approved tiger. Wolf and warg mounts use the same approved art, with the existing rider gear and darker warg coat.

The wolf mount adapter (`PX.wolf.mount` / `PX.mountArt`) supports the existing Wolf Rider, Wolf Archer and Warg Raider sprites, including loose saddled mounts and authored fallen poses. The saddle follows the moving back; templates are cloned before tack or barding is drawn.

Alpha wolves (`alpha_wolf`) use every approved wolf animation at 125% size. Newly spawned wild packs have a 25% chance of one stronger leader replacing a regular wolf; its mixed roster, survival and rewards persist in saves. Wolf and warg riders use this larger body in a 40 × 36 mount frame, with centered tack and a saddle that follows the back. Tunables are in `data/monsters.js` (`WOLF_ALPHA`, `ST.alpha_wolf`); coverage is in `tests/alphawolf.js` and `tests/wolfmount.js`.

Monster breeds (2026-11-12; the owner: "remove eye from slimes, and add more monster variations"): `data/monsters.js` `MONSTER_BREEDS`. A lair may be one of its kind's breeds, by the ground it lies on (`world/monsters.js` `breedAt`: a hash of where it lies, no draw; the kind's own weighs 1 on its own ground). Eleven: frost and ember slimes (the frost, the desert), snow spiders (snowy woods), blood widows, ironhide boars (hills), the ice bear, frost and bog trolls, black scorpions, the snow griffin and the ice wyrm. Each has its own fighter (its kind's changed: `ST[id].breed`), name, lore, xp, trophy (new: Rime Jelly, Ember Jelly, Ice Bear Pelt) and drops; its damage type, resistances and tags its own where given. Its figure is its kind's in its own coats (`engine/beasts.js` `PX.breed`), on the map too; its deaths, mind, mass, moves and portrait are its kind's (`breedsInto` fills those tables). A lair keeps `breed` in its save; `monOf(m)` answers for its unit, name and loot; a bounty on one names it. The wolf and the tiger are painted, so they have none. `MONSTER_BREEDS.on` false: no breeds (the fingerprints' proof). The wiki lists them under Monsters. The slime has no eyes now, on its sprite and its portrait. Tests: breeds.

Three old failures fixed (2026-11-12; the owner: "check on those"). The wiki (`wiki.html`) built again: its hero section reads today's trees (one for each proficiency, `PROF_TREE`; what a past starts you ahead in; the checks that once named an attribute), a holding with no realm (a free hamlet) no longer stops it, and its realm map is drawn from the minimap with a square for each holding in its realm's colour (it painted the whole world and built every lot's buildings: 39 s to load, now about 4). A ram killed under its rider lies on the ground (`gfx/sprites.js` `mountDown`: its own lying art, the belly death slumped, `engine/beasts.js` `PX.mountArt`; laid down from its standing figure it floated). A lord who has nothing to offer you still says why while you are shut out of his work (`world/quests.js` `lordJobHtml`). Tests: wiki, deaths, quests.

Wolf artwork is restored from `7ce306d`, preserving its approved head, fur and proportions across all poses. Alpha frames render directly from that master at the larger combat size, avoiding a second enlargement of reduced pixels. Alpha leaders, pack spawning, saves and centered rider mounts remain.

Wolf and warg riders sit into centered saddles, with a forward thigh, bent knee and shin angled back (`people.js` `RIDER_POSTURE`). The moving back carries the same saddle, rider and equipment alignment through walking, running and attacks.

Wolf/warg gallops have a stronger suspension lift and a forward rider lean. `drawMountedRider` keeps the hips seated and dresses the shifted upper body afterwards, preserving native weapon shapes and hand anchors. Walking has a lighter lean; idle remains upright.

All mounts now share the seated knee/shin pose and forward rider lean (`people.js` `RIDER_POSTURE`, `RIDER_LEG`, `drawMountedRider`). This covers horse, stag, ram, wolf and warg riders and mounted heroes. Each mount keeps its authored gait and saddle motion. `tests/mountposture.js` verifies the common posture and anchors across all five types.

Ridden rams use a smooth saddle patch around the waist and thighs so wool does not obscure the rider. The patch follows each gait and preserves the ram silhouette; loose rams keep their fleece. `mountposture` checks the bounded clearance area and stable seat in all nine idle/walk/run frames.

Dwarf portrait anatomy is redesigned in the shared generation engine (`engine/face arts/grayscale/portrait-engine.js`): a compact broad skull, square jaw, heavy brows, wider nose, stout neck and fuller beard silhouettes. Both bearded and unbearded faces retain dwarf proportions. The workshop and game face maker use the same renderer, preserving saved recipe choices and seeded generation. See `doc/DWARF_PORTRAIT_DESIGN.md`.

Dwarf portrait necks are shortened by seating the entire head eight pixels closer to the shoulders, with the collar fixed. Facial parts, beards, helmets and exported anchors stay aligned without rescaling the artwork.

The portrait generator adds four helmets (horned war helm, dwarf horned/rune helms and elven winged helm) and four armor sets (dwarf runic/forge plate and elven silver scale/leaf cuirass). They appear in compatible selectors, five editable presets and military NPC generation pools. Metal, clothing and headwear tints remain independent. Worn-gear portrait rules recognize the new parts; these are portrait choices. See `doc/PORTRAIT_ARMOR_DESIGN.md`.

Dwarf portrait engravings now vary across eight coordinated motif families, selectable under Armor engraving in the workshop. Existing seeds preserve the chosen motif through recipes and saved portraits. Rune/forge armor, mail and dwarf helms use the new marks. Both offline portrait tools are rebuilt to match the shared renderer.

Dwarf portraits gain ten facial options: three heavy brows, two narrow/deep-set eyes, broad-bridge/broken noses, fan beard, twin braids and heavy moustache. They appear in the face maker, four editable presets and named dwarf generation pools. Short-neck proportions remain. Moustache-only portraits avoid a full beard on battle figures; both offline portrait tools are rebuilt.


Portrait pixel style (2026-11-10): the shared portrait engine now draws the approved coarser skin, hair and equipment treatment in the face maker and named-character portraits. Facial features keep native pixel contours, ear roots have subtle contact shading, and orc heads/helmets are 12.5% smaller (25% for the wide `orc-heavy` infantry head), with facial detail redrawn at that size. Saved choices and tint channels remain. The workshop and both offline tools use the same renderer. Design and checks: `doc/PIXEL_PORTRAIT_DESIGN.md`, `engine/face arts/grayscale/pixel-tests.html`.


Orc portrait anatomy (2026-11-10): compatible orc faces now have a broad flat chin and lower jaw, stronger brow ridges, flattened wide noses, heavier lower lips and taller tusks. The smaller head/helmet proportions stay in place; facial options and sliders remain editable in the shared generator.


Approved orc portrait study: the shared generator now uses the rounder jaw, mirrored healthy parts, narrow eyes and taller mohawk. Configured injuries remain asymmetric, and head sizes stay reduced. Eight leather/harness options include six covered kits and two bare harnesses, with straps reaching the shoulder tops. They appear in the face maker, presets and compatible NPC role pools; worn-gear classification recognizes the new kits. A separate torso-skin layer keeps skin, leather and metal tints independent. Both offline tools are rebuilt. Design and checks: `doc/ORC_PORTRAIT_DESIGN.md`, `engine/face arts/grayscale/orc-tests.html`.


Shared portrait hairstyles: all 38 styles are selectable for all races, including mohawks, forge braids, braided crowns, knight crops and tonsures. The engine weights random choices by race, with every style still possible; game appearance rules retain chosen hairstyles instead of forcing dwarf braids. Race changes in the face maker preserve the selected cut. Anatomy, beard, bald-frequency and color preferences remain. Checks: `engine/face arts/grayscale/hair-tests.html`, game `faces`.

Closed portrait helmets: twelve face-covering styles are selectable for every race, with clear vision slits and fitted jaw coverage. Hair, ears and beards tuck inside while remaining in the saved recipe. The highest worn-helmet tier uses a closed knight helm, dwarf visor, elven sallet or orc war mask. Military portrait pools and both standalone tools include the styles; inventory and combat stats are unchanged. Checks: portrait `helmet-tests.html`, `pixel-tests.html` and game `faces`.

Full helmet refinement: rounded cheek/jaw bowls, beveled great-helm corners and curved rear skirts soften the silhouettes. Pixel-sharp visor shadows darken the exposed eyes only while the front helmet is visible; source eye layers and facial choices remain intact.

Reference-based closed visors now use long, narrow brow slits with a stepped upward slope toward a solid nose bridge. A raised center seam and paired breathing holes define the faceplate; deeper cavity shading keeps the eyes recessed. Apertures remain sharp on the two-pixel grid and keep pupil anchors clear.

Full helmet lighting: a single curved crown glint and broad rounded light/shadow regions define the metal volume. A darker steel silhouette separates the helmet from the background. Rivets, reinforcement bands, flutes, leaf ribs and vents use connected native-scale marks. Headwear tint stays editable; all details use the shared equipment palette and two-pixel grid.

The game's own faces follow the approved orc study (2026-11-10; `ui/faces.js` `FACE_KIN.orc`, `FACE_UNIT`): orc troops wear its leather and harness kits by their armour (leather: the laced vest, studded leather or shoulder guards; hide: layered hide or a harness over leather; bare: a bare harness; a reaver a bare crossed harness, a wolf rider shoulder guards), goblins too; and every people may wear them (the owner: "leather or harness can be wore by everyone": the engine's race limit on the eight kits lifted, `engine/face arts/grayscale/portrait-engine.js`; the torso's skin takes each people's tint): a human's, dwarf's or elf's leather a gambeson or one of the kits by the face's seed, hide fur or layered hide, so a militiaman, a dwarf shieldbearer or a highwayman may wear studded leather or shoulder guards; those orcs in mail or plate have the heavy orc face (its smaller head). Orc hair uses the shared hairstyle catalog with racial preferences; a flat broad nose, an orc's ears, tusks, at most a short bristle. Your own face and a companion's stay the engine's own (`world: true`: you shape yours). Tests: portraits.

Full helmet variations: barrel, rivet-banded, fluted knight, horned closed, leaf visor and tusked closed helms add six shared choices and six presets. All twelve keep fitted face coverage, narrow shaded visor slits, paired detail and the same pixel-scale steel reflections. The face maker, military role pools and both offline workshops expose them; racial generation preferences favor the horned, leaf and tusked designs. Checks: portrait `helmet-tests.html`, `pixel-tests.html`, character generation, game `faces` and `portraits`.

Portrait equipment quality: scale, lamellar, plate, leaf armor, forge emblems, leather lacing and embroidered trim gain deliberate detail on the final two-pixel grid. Existing coverage, shoulder fits, face parts and approved helmet art remain intact; material tints stay independent. The game, raw part exports and both standalone workshops use the same art. Checks: portrait `equipment-tests.html`, `pixel-tests.html`, part preservation, game `faces` and workshop recipe round trips.

Full helmet contour cleanup: great-helm crowns and reinforcing bands follow curved metal profiles, with tapered center straps and jaw contours. A redundant straight-sided face lining is removed after coverage checks. Broad curved lighting regions replace the patchy source shading. Deliberate native-scale vents, rivets and ribs retain sharp pixel edges. Both helmet layers use the shared steel palette; checks cover all races for detached grey blocks, clear visors and fitted coverage.

Helmet lighting and contour redraw: a shaded steel exterior edge follows both helmet layers and excludes enclosed visor holes. Smoothly curved light/shadow regions and one crown reflection replace the small disconnected grey patches. Construction details are drawn directly on the native two-pixel grid. Face coverage, visor apertures, body art and other portrait parts keep their existing geometry.

Helmet edge shading follows neighboring metal about one palette step darker, blending lit edges into the crown and keeping deeper steel on shadowed edges. Exterior contours avoid near-black; visor cavities and breathing holes retain their deeper shadows.

Closed helmet backing fits inside the final front-shell mask, including at the cheek, jaw and neck. This removes the exposed grey lining crescents and double lower contours. The assembled portrait has a single jaw edge; head shape, front details and armor retain their placement. Raw backing remains a separate layer. The barrel reinforcing strap ends above the cheek vents, and the horned helm omits the lower stroke that crossed them. Fit checks enforce containment at all proportion extremes.

Three helmet polish studies: the barrel great helm has a lower rolled crown, fuller cheek profile and a cylindrical reflection; the fluted knight helm has a taller peaked crown and four broad ribs; the horned closed helm has a low forged crown, thicker swept horn roots and a reinforced brow. Each has a different vent arrangement and material highlight. These three now render the shell/ornament geometry first, followed by material lighting and native-scale construction details, avoiding a redundant earlier decoration pass. Their blended steel contours and fitted backing remain. Other helmet styles keep the approved art. Review includes 128, 80 and 44-pixel portraits on olive, dark and light backgrounds.

Campaign HUD: `campaign-hud.css` and `js/ui/campaignhud.js` provide a shared skin for desktop and phone. `ui/icons.js` uses the transparent `art/ui/hud-icons.png` atlas; the open nine-slice rim also has alpha transparency. Timber belongs to the panel surface, and icon artwork has no opaque backdrop. See `doc/CAMPAIGN_UI_DESIGN.md`.

The bottom-left panel header shows the army name and actual flag above a subtle divider; resources share the bottom-left panel with conditions in two icon columns. Low supplies are emphasized there and open inventory. The original clock and shortcuts remain, with unframed speed buttons inside the time strip. The larger expandable company card is portrait-free and retains full party details. Bottom commands are slimmer, separately framed tiles; the full-width bottom backdrop is removed, leaving the world visible and clickable between panels. Settings/Menu leads the far-left utilities in the top rail. The 44px desktop top rail fits its 36px controls with 4px vertical margins; they share frames and type. Company and region headings, labels and values follow the same typography and casing; the upper-right minimap has zoom buttons on its frame and replaces the desktop Map command. A raised, framed region section projects above the center of the desktop top rail, using the same forged-rim artwork and timber surface as the bottom-left panel, tapered into a trapezoid with adaptive width. It shows the current territory, realm and terrain only inside discovered claimed land; hovering or targeting another holding cannot change it. Fitness, morale and stamina share one illustrated-icon/value/gauge component and Arial typography. Landscape phones keep a 52px strip, 44px controls and icon-only navigation; their status, speed and map drawers share the desktop materials. Day/weather has leading padding on both layouts.

Map frames hug the live minimap canvas without a wooden spacer. Console sections use common safe margins and have no gray end strips. `tests/campaignhud.js` covers controls, data and map alignment; `tests/mobilehud.js` also checks shared condition widgets, expanded meter width and the compact touch layout.

Holding banners above map buildings are temporarily disabled by `HOLD_PLAQUE.on` in `gfx/structures.js`; the top-bar region section remains. The artwork is retained for later use.

The desktop company panel aligns Supplies and Condition on a shared row grid, with inset segmented meters, distinct muted condition colors, a center divider and a framed Details control in the final right-hand row.

Shared interface icon family: `ui/icons.js` owns five transparent atlases (the approved HUD sheet plus `art/ui/icons-interface.png`, `icons-trade.png`, `icons-equipment.png` and `icons-supplies.png`). One hundred new illustrations cover the remaining menus, weather, places, goods and equipment. All shared UI symbols, 54 inventory categories, orders, abilities, status badges, troop types and talent-node symbols use this family. Inventory material swatches preserve item colours, and drag ghosts carry the same illustrated item; battle badges retain their status rims and accessible words. Formation diagrams keep their actual layouts with metal studs. `tests/uiicons.js` checks coverage, crops, alpha, semantic distinctions and integration; focused fieldbook, inventory, army and battle UI suites cover existing controls.

Portrait reference art pass (2026-11-12): the shared grayscale portrait engine now uses the original `hum_sword.png` lighting and material style while retaining the front-facing composition, rig and editable recipes. Skin, hair and garment contours retain native detail; steel reflections, cloth and skin use distinct tint ramps. The game face maker, troops and named characters inherit the renderer. Offline workshops are rebuilt. Checks: portrait pixel/material checks, layer and character checks, race/equipment checks, game `faces` and `portraits`.

Shared button feedback (2026-11-13): `campaign-hud.css` supplies hover, held press, selected/expanded, keyboard-focus and disabled lighting for the illustrated controls throughout the game. `ui/input.js` retains quick click feedback for 180ms and follows stable ids across redraws without changing control actions. Phone taps use the same states without sticky hover; reduced-motion mode removes movement and transitions. Time-strip selection uses a warm inset glow and brighter text, without an underline or nested frame. Tests: `buttonstates`, `campaignhud`, `mobilehud`, plus real mouse/keyboard/touch review.

Phone speed selection: the compact speed button fills warmly while its drawer is open; selected speed options retain their full forged frames. There is no extra inner underline or selection stripe.

The phone speed picker is a compact 192px popover joined directly to its main speed button, with four 44px targets. It follows that control across orientation changes rather than spanning the full HUD.


Painted portrait components (2026-11-13): `engine/face arts/painted/` supplies 50 local authored masters fitted into the current 19 dynamic portrait layers. `painted-data.js` embeds the source PNGs; `painted-engine.js` follows the existing geometry engine in both game entry points. `Faces.url` uses the painted renderer at 256 pixels, refreshing any loading-time fallback portraits when art is ready. Saved recipes, race choices, equipment fit and character identities are unchanged. The full offline NPC studio and focused artwork editor expose actual painted layer exports. Checks: `paintedportraits` (all races and eye/mouth options, independent parts/tints and fit), `faces`, `portraits`, `structure`, offline studio and collection round trips.

Facial artwork refinement (2026-11-13): shared painted eye/mouth envelopes avoid vertical texture ripples; orc lips follow skin color, and both tusk sizes use pointed mirrored ivory shading with attached roots. Nose bridges join the forehead without a cut-color seam. The game and both portable portrait editors share the correction. `paintedportraits` covers variant identity, tusk silhouette/shading/fit and nose-root seams.

Painted skin details (2026-11-13): aging, freckles, sunspots, pockmarks and scars in `painted/painted-engine.js` now follow the local face texture/shading rather than fixed tinted gray strokes. Age folds gain shallow relief and subtle eye-bag volume; scar rims and pigment stay subdued. Age/mark layers, choices and skin colors remain dynamic in game and portable editors. `paintedportraits` checks local contrast and eye/skin containment on dark/light palettes across races, preset distinctness and clean young/unmarked faces.

Portrait neckline fit (2026-11-13): painted body materials now skip the source artwork’s transparent neck opening and align with the actual collar edge. This removes blocky strips below the neck while retaining skin/jaw art, source material scale and existing fit masks. Both offline editors are rebuilt. `paintedportraits` checks eight outfits across profiles and face/jaw extremes, including bare harnesses.

Portrait skin containment (2026-11-13): the painted face respects source transparency and a dedicated jaw/neck silhouette, removing the padded lower rim. Short stubble stays inside the cleaned face mask. The cleanup only removes old coverage; it preserves rig anchors, recipes and fitted clothing. `paintedportraits` checks containment, connected skin and no stubble spill across races, profiles and face/jaw width extremes.

Portrait contour follow-up (2026-11-13): lower cheek/jaw coverage now tapers inward, and curved neck side paths replace the earlier rectangular join. This addresses the broad strip still visible in the prior cleanup preview. Portrait and export checks accompany a same-recipe, same-scale visual comparison; passing mask containment alone is not visual approval.

Shared portrait skin edge (2026-11-13): upper-neck torso skin now shares the cleaned face outline instead of protruding behind a trimmed jaw. Lower-face attached edge pixels receive a small mask cleanup. Age, marks and short stubble follow that same edge; bare chest skin below the shoulder line stays independent. `paintedportraits` now checks the assembled skin layers across races/profiles/widths.

Original portrait outline restored (2026-11-13): custom jaw tapering, neck reshaping and erosion are removed. The original rig plus authored skin alpha now define a clipping-only boundary. Retained skin RGB/placement and jaw anchors match the original pre-reshaping renderer; torso neck, age, marks and short stubble share that boundary. The body/chest and long beard shapes remain independent. This supersedes the preceding silhouette experiments.

Portrait base-component repair (2026-11-13): actual skin bounds prevent a repeated first-row crown tab. Head-field preparation excludes baked-in ears; separate ear parts remain independent. The male human source’s wider neck backing is clipped outside the existing painted jaw edge. Source PNGs and jaw anchors stay unchanged. `paintedportraits` checks crowns at native/production size and independent ear selection; the blank-head preview shows the actual repaired parts.
